* **Command:** "Hi Apogee please answer this question  In today's current society especially in Kenya the entertainment industry especially event planning has been mediocre and consumers are complaining that they are not getting a bang for their buck For instance the recently concluded Most Wanted festival had a lot of failure points including ticket failures lack of communication for the wristbands as well as lack of quality control especially for crowds in the event what are some of the current loopholes in the event planning industry and what key points can be looked forward to to ensure that quality entertainment can be provided to consumers not only in concerts but in future endeavors that require brand identities I teach this as a novel idea not necessarily something that is in the vault feel free to use the Internet to get your information and your reasoning"
* **Action:** "Good question, and it's a real gap right now, not just a Kenya problem but definitely amplified here given how fast the events scene has grown without the operational systems catching up.

Here's what's actually failing at events like Most Wanted, based on common patterns in the industry:

Ticketing and access control tend to break down because organizers use disconnected systems, one platform sells tickets, another handles verification at the gate, and there's no real-time sync. That's how you get people with valid tickets stuck outside or duplicate entries getting through.

Communication is usually an afterthought. Wristband distribution, gate times, re-entry rules, these get announced once on social media and then chaos happens on the ground because there's no dedicated info channel or on-site signage plan.

Crowd control fails because promoters underestimate density versus venue capacity, and they don't hire enough trained personnel who know crowd flow, not just security in the traditional sense.

The fix points that consistently separate good events from bad ones: a single integrated ticketing and access system tested before doors open, a dedicated operations lead separate from the marketing team, clear signage and a live communication channel like SMS or WhatsApp broadcast for real-time updates, and capacity planning done against the actual venue layout, not just ticket sales targets.

If brands want to build trust for future events, the consistency of execution matters more than one flashy lineup. One clean, well-run mid-size event builds more loyalty than a big one that falls apart.

Since you flagged this as a novel idea outside the vault, want me to log it as a hypothesis so we can build a validation path if you want to explore this as an actual venture?"

Four, a close-up detail shot. Just the diaper itself, showing the fit and thinness against the baby's skin or clothing, three to five seconds, steady hand, good light.

Keep each clip short, raw, and unedited — don't trim them yourself, send raw footage. Do you want me to also give you a one-line voice-over script for each, in case you go that route instead of live audio?"

* **Action:** "Good question — this one's easy to solve with angle and timing rather than editing.

A few practical tips: frame it so the shot is mostly you, your hands, and the diaper itself, with the baby's torso and legs visible but the diaper change happening slightly off to the edge of frame or partially blocked by your arm. You can also time it so the camera catches the motion right before the diaper's off and right after the new one's on, rather than the fully open moment. That gives you the "authentic" feel the brief wants without anything explicit.

If you want, I can give you a similar tasteful angle suggestion for the reveal moment in shot three as well."

## Observed customer evidence

### Customer Interview #001
1. Has ever wished they had perfume/fragrance when they didn't? **YES.**
2. Last time / what happened? They used soap under their armpits.
3. What did they do instead? Used soap under their armpits.
4. How much of an inconvenience? **"Not much."**
5. Bought perfume mainly for a particular occasion? **NO.**
	- Customer explanation: "I buy a scent that I would like to augment myself for a while, I usually have 1-2 colognes at a time. I have one longtime favourite."
6. Wanted to try a fragrance before buying the whole bottle? **YES.**
	- Customer explanation: "Yes I am more likely to buy if I have a sampler that I have time to get to like."
7. Reaction to the vending concept / where would they expect it?
	- Customer suggested: "At the club or at a place where many university students go."
	- Customer added: "Most men already know how they want to smell. I think?"
8. Where would they expect to see it? Club or a place where many university students go.
9. What would make them actually use it? "A really good scent at the club."
10. What would make them not use it? "Being sober."
11. Reasonable price? "Really good cologne maybe 250kshs for a spray."
	- Interpretation note: Record **250kshs** exactly as stated. It is not yet clear whether this means KSh 250 per spray or something else; clarify in a future interview.
12. Expected fragrances? "Top notch hard to find in Kenya."

### Customer Interview #002
1. Yes.
2. Rushed through traffic for a meeting using a matatu and had to alight and walk to get to the office.
3. Washed my armpits with washroom soap and hand towels.
4. Got late but better late than stinky.
5. No, not particularly for an occasion but always have perfume on anytime.
6. Yes.
7. Yes, great idea!! Seen it with one of my friends during a night out.
8. Gym, night clubs.
9. Feeling of freshness all round.
10. Sometimes a scent should be mysterious or 2 scents that do not compliment you.
11. Is it a whole bottle or just a splash? Splash at 100 KShs.
12. As per market trends — replicate perfume shops; only with a closeness/convenience to where the client is.

### Customer Interview #003
1. Has ever wished they had perfume/fragrance when they didn't? **NO.**
2. Last time / what happened? **N/A.**
3. What did they do instead? **"If it happened, I would just make sure I had deodorant."**
4. How much of an inconvenience? **N/A.**
5. Bought perfume mainly for a particular occasion? **YES.**
6. Wanted to try a fragrance before buying the whole bottle? **YES.**
	- Customer explanation: **"Yes, always test first, even if I have the fragrance before."**
7-12. **Not answered.**

## Derived observations (source interpretation)

### Customer Interview #001
- There is evidence of occasional desire for fragrance when the customer does not have it.
- The customer explicitly said the inconvenience was not significant.
- The customer is already a fragrance owner/user and usually keeps 1-2 colognes.
- Sampling appears more compelling to this customer than emergency fragrance access: they said they are more likely to buy after having time with a sampler.
- The club appears more compelling than a generic high-foot-traffic location.
- "Really good scent" appears important to usage.
- Premium/hard-to-find fragrances may be more attractive than ordinary fragrances.
- The customer's "being sober" answer may indicate a nightlife/social-context hypothesis, but this needs testing and is not a customer fact.
- The stated 250kshs price needs clarification because it is unusually specific and may represent a misunderstanding of the question.

### Customer Interview #002
- Problem strength: Stronger signal than Customer #001 because this respondent experienced a real situation where they needed fragrance, used a workaround, and was willing to be late rather than arrive smelling bad. This remains evidence from one respondent, not validation.
- Existing behavior: Strong perfume usage; the respondent says they always have perfume on.
- Sampling: Confirmed interest.
- Concept interest: Positive, but the respondent had already seen something similar with a friend during a night out, so this is not independent proof of demand.
- Venue signal: Gym and nightclubs.
- Value proposition signal: Freshness plus convenience/proximity.
- Fragrance concern: Poor scent selection or incompatible combinations could reduce use.
- WTP: The respondent stated "Splash at 100 KShs." Record this as CUSTOMER EVIDENCE / UNVALIDATED, not validated pricing.
- Assortment signal: The respondent favors following market trends / perfume-shop selection, with convenience being the differentiator.
- Overall evidence strength: TWO CUSTOMERS - still insufficient to validate the business.

### Customer Interview #003
- Customer 3 does not validate the core problem of needing fragrance while away from home.
- Deodorant is an alternative behavior for this customer if the situation occurs.
- Occasion-driven fragrance purchasing is supported by this interview.
- Sampling is a strong behavior signal: the customer always tests first, including fragrances they have used before.
- No inference can be made yet about willingness to use, pay for, or seek out a fragrance vending machine because Q7-Q12 were not answered.
- This evidence should remain separate from the conclusions drawn from Customers 1 and 2.

- Problem strength: WEAK / UNVALIDATED
- Customer interest: INTERESTING SIGNAL
- Willingness to pay: UNVALIDATED - price needs clarification
- Venue signal: CLUB / UNIVERSITY-STUDENT ENVIRONMENT HYPOTHESIS
- Fragrance signal: PREMIUM / HARD-TO-FIND FRAGRANCES
- Sampling signal: INTERESTING SIGNAL
- Overall: ONE CUSTOMER - INSUFFICIENT EVIDENCE

### Customer Interview #002
- Problem strength: STRONGER SIGNAL THAN #001, but still UNVALIDATED
- Existing behavior: STRONG PERFUME USAGE SIGNAL
- Sampling: CONFIRMED INTEREST
- Concept interest: POSITIVE INTEREST; prior exposure noted
- Venue signal: GYM / NIGHTCLUB HYPOTHESIS
- Value proposition signal: FRESHNESS + CONVENIENCE/PROXIMITY
- Fragrance signal: SELECTION QUALITY / COMPATIBILITY CONCERN
- Willingness to pay: KSh 100 for a splash - CUSTOMER EVIDENCE / UNVALIDATED
- Assortment signal: MARKET-TREND / PERFUME-SHOP SELECTION HYPOTHESIS
- Overall: TWO CUSTOMERS - INSUFFICIENT EVIDENCE

- Emergency fragrance problem: NO SUPPORT FROM THIS CUSTOMER
- Alternative behavior: DEODORANT
- Occasion-driven purchase: CUSTOMER EVIDENCE / ONE CUSTOMER
- Sampling: STRONG CUSTOMER SIGNAL / ONE CUSTOMER
- Vending-machine interest or willingness to pay: NOT TESTED
- Overall: THREE CUSTOMERS - INSUFFICIENT EVIDENCE; DO NOT CHANGE PRODUCT DIRECTION

1. Do other customers also say the club is the strongest use case?

	- Customer explanation: **"Yes, always test first, even if I have the fragrance before."**
7-12. **Not answered.**

## Derived observations (source interpretation)

### Customer Interview #001
- There is evidence of occasional desire for fragrance when the customer does not have it.
- The customer explicitly said the inconvenience was not significant.
- The customer is already a fragrance owner/user and usually keeps 1-2 colognes.
- Sampling appears more compelling to this customer than emergency fragrance access: they said they are more likely to buy after having time with a sampler.
- The club appears more compelling than a generic high-foot-traffic location.
- "Really good scent" appears important to usage.
- Premium/hard-to-find fragrances may be more attractive than ordinary fragrances.
- The customer's "being sober" answer may indicate a nightlife/social-context hypothesis, but this needs testing and is not a customer fact.
- The stated 250kshs price needs clarification because it is unusually specific and may represent a misunderstanding of the question.

### Customer Interview #002
- Problem strength: Stronger signal than Customer #001 because this respondent experienced a real situation where they needed fragrance, used a workaround, and was willing to be late rather than arrive smelling bad. This remains evidence from one respondent, not validation.
- Existing behavior: Strong perfume usage; the respondent says they always have perfume on.
- Sampling: Confirmed interest.
- Concept interest: Positive, but the respondent had already seen something similar with a friend during a night out, so this is not independent proof of demand.
- Venue signal: Gym and nightclubs.
- Value proposition signal: Freshness plus convenience/proximity.
- Fragrance concern: Poor scent selection or incompatible combinations could reduce use.
- WTP: The respondent stated "Splash at 100 KShs." Record this as CUSTOMER EVIDENCE / UNVALIDATED, not validated pricing.
- Assortment signal: The respondent favors following market trends / perfume-shop selection, with convenience being the differentiator.
- Overall evidence strength: TWO CUSTOMERS - still insufficient to validate the business.

### Customer Interview #003
- Customer 3 does not validate the core problem of needing fragrance while away from home.
- Deodorant is an alternative behavior for this customer if the situation occurs.
- Occasion-driven fragrance purchasing is supported by this interview.
- Sampling is a strong behavior signal: the customer always tests first, including fragrances they have used before.
- No inference can be made yet about willingness to use, pay for, or seek out a fragrance vending machine because Q7-Q12 were not answered.
- This evidence should remain separate from the conclusions drawn from Customers 1 and 2.

### Customer Interview #003
1. Has ever wished they had perfume/fragrance when they didn't? **NO.**
2. Last time / what happened? **N/A.**
3. What did they do instead? **"If it happened, I would just make sure I had deodorant."**
4. How much of an inconvenience? **N/A.**
5. Bought perfume mainly for a particular occasion? **YES.**
6. Wanted to try a fragrance before buying the whole bottle? **YES.**
	- Customer explanation: **"Yes, always test first, even if I have the fragrance before."**
7. **"Good idea.... seems costly to manage"**
8. **"Barber shop, Gym, offices, high end bathrooms, night clubs and airports."**
9. **"Only if I forget or need a top up... ."**
10. **"depends on costing"**
11. **"Have to calculate amount in on spray, based on the total ounce price."**
12. **"Long lasting top end fragrances..."**

### Customer Interview #004
1. **Yes**
2. **A random meet up that required showing up proper after already exhausting the day**
3. **Showered**
4. **Not really big, but its a difficult feeling to shake off knowing you aren't at your best**
5. **Yes**
6. **Most definitely, i have to choose what smells I like, its not about the brand or amount, its what I find appeasing.**
7-12. **Not answered.**

### Customer Interview #005
1. Has ever wished they had perfume/fragrance when they didn't? **Yes.**
2. Last time / what happened? **I forgot to spray, and I'm already on my way to work or a function. Skipped a session in my morning routine because I was moving too quick.**
3. What did they do instead? **I had to get some deo stick at a store or some Axe spray.**
4. How much of an inconvenience? **Spent money I didn't mean to and spent time driving around looking for a store, and had to settle for a scent that may not be mine. Also, I'm stuck with a spray I didn't particularly like that much. I was also smelling my own musky scent before solving the problem. If I can smell myself, then people can too.**
5. Bought perfume mainly for a particular occasion? **Yes.** The reason is **"aura farm"** — I have to be introduced by my scent before I show up; they smell you before they see you.
6. Wanted to try a fragrance before buying the whole bottle? **That's the biggest issue with scents; you have to smell it before buying it, and technology hasn't solved that for online shopping. You buy based on others' opinions, which might not be your opinion.**
7-12. **Not answered.**

## Derived observations (source interpretation)

### Customer Interview #001
- There is evidence of occasional desire for fragrance when the customer does not have it.
- The customer explicitly said the inconvenience was not significant.
- The customer is already a fragrance owner/user and usually keeps 1-2 colognes.
- Sampling appears more compelling to this customer than emergency fragrance access: they said they are more likely to buy after having time with a sampler.
- The club appears more compelling than a generic high-foot-traffic location.
- "Really good scent" appears important to usage.
- Premium/hard-to-find fragrances may be more attractive than ordinary fragrances.
- The customer's "being sober" answer may indicate a nightlife/social-context hypothesis, but this needs testing and is not a customer fact.
- The stated 250kshs price needs clarification because it is unusually specific and may represent a misunderstanding of the question.

### Customer Interview #002
- Problem strength: Stronger signal than Customer #001 because this respondent experienced a real situation where they needed fragrance, used a workaround, and was willing to be late rather than arrive smelling bad. This remains evidence from one respondent, not validation.
- Existing behavior: Strong perfume usage; the respondent says they always have perfume on.
- Sampling: Confirmed interest.
- Concept interest: Positive, but the respondent had already seen something similar with a friend during a night out, so this is not independent proof of demand.
- Venue signal: Gym and nightclubs.
- Value proposition signal: Freshness plus convenience/proximity.
- Fragrance concern: Poor scent selection or incompatible combinations could reduce use.
- WTP: The respondent stated "Splash at 100 KShs." Record this as CUSTOMER EVIDENCE / UNVALIDATED, not validated pricing.
- Assortment signal: The respondent favors following market trends / perfume-shop selection, with convenience being the differentiator.
- Overall evidence strength: TWO CUSTOMERS - still insufficient to validate the business.

### Customer Interview #003
- Customer 3 does not validate the core problem of needing fragrance while away from home.
- Deodorant is an alternative behavior for this customer if the situation occurs.
- Occasion-driven fragrance purchasing is supported by this interview.
- Sampling is a strong behavior signal: the customer always tests first, including fragrances they have used before.
- No inference can be made yet about willingness to use, pay for, or seek out a fragrance vending machine because Q7-Q12 were not answered.
- This evidence should remain separate from the conclusions drawn from Customers 1 and 2.

## Derived observations (source interpretation)

### Customer Interview #001
- There is evidence of occasional desire for fragrance when the customer does not have it.
- The customer explicitly said the inconvenience was not significant.
- The customer is already a fragrance owner/user and usually keeps 1-2 colognes.
- Sampling appears more compelling to this customer than emergency fragrance access: they said they are more likely to buy after having time with a sampler.
- The club appears more compelling than a generic high-foot-traffic location.
- "Really good scent" appears important to usage.
- Premium/hard-to-find fragrances may be more attractive than ordinary fragrances.
- The customer's "being sober" answer may indicate a nightlife/social-context hypothesis, but this needs testing and is not a customer fact.
- The stated 250kshs price needs clarification because it is unusually specific and may represent a misunderstanding of the question.

### Customer Interview #002
- Problem strength: Stronger signal than Customer #001 because this respondent experienced a real situation where they needed fragrance, used a workaround, and was willing to be late rather than arrive smelling bad. This remains evidence from one respondent, not validation.
- Existing behavior: Strong perfume usage; the respondent says they always have perfume on.
- Sampling: Confirmed interest.
- Concept interest: Positive, but the respondent had already seen something similar with a friend during a night out, so this is not independent proof of demand.
- Venue signal: Gym and nightclubs.
- Value proposition signal: Freshness plus convenience/proximity.
- Fragrance concern: Poor scent selection or incompatible combinations could reduce use.
- WTP: The respondent stated "Splash at 100 KShs." Record this as CUSTOMER EVIDENCE / UNVALIDATED, not validated pricing.
- Assortment signal: The respondent favors following market trends / perfume-shop selection, with convenience being the differentiator.
- Overall evidence strength: TWO CUSTOMERS - still insufficient to validate the business.

### Customer Interview #003
- Customer 3 does not validate the core problem of needing fragrance while away from home.
- Deodorant is an alternative behavior for this customer if the situation occurs.
- Occasion-driven fragrance purchasing is supported by this interview.
- Sampling is a strong behavior signal: the customer always tests first, including fragrances they have used before.
- No inference can be made yet about willingness to use, pay for, or seek out a fragrance vending machine because Q7-Q12 were not answered.
- This evidence should remain separate from the conclusions drawn from Customers 1 and 2.

7. **"Good idea.... seems costly to manage"**
8. **"Barber shop, Gym, offices, high end bathrooms, night clubs and airports."**
9. **"Only if I forget or need a top up... ."**
10. **"depends on costing"**
11. **"Have to calculate amount in on spray, based on the total ounce price."**
12. **"Long lasting top end fragrances..."**

### Customer Interview #004
1. **Yes**
2. **A random meet up that required showing up proper after already exhausting the day**
3. **Showered**
4. **Not really big, but its a difficult feeling to shake off knowing you aren't at your best**
5. **Yes**
6. **Most definitely, i have to choose what smells I like, its not about the brand or amount, its what I find appeasing.**
7-12. **Not answered.**

### Customer Interview #005
1. Has ever wished they had perfume/fragrance when they didn't? **Yes.**
2. Last time / what happened? **I forgot to spray, and I'm already on my way to work or a function. Skipped a session in my morning routine because I was moving too quick.**
3. What did they do instead? **I had to get some deo stick at a store or some Axe spray.**
4. How much of an inconvenience? **Spent money I didn't mean to and spent time driving around looking for a store, and had to settle for a scent that may not be mine. Also, I'm stuck with a spray I didn't particularly like that much. I was also smelling my own musky scent before solving the problem. If I can smell myself, then people can too.**
5. Bought perfume mainly for a particular occasion? **Yes.** The reason is **"aura farm"** — I have to be introduced by my scent before I show up; they smell you before they see you.
6. Wanted to try a fragrance before buying the whole bottle? **That's the biggest issue with scents; you have to smell it before buying it, and technology hasn't solved that for online shopping. You buy based on others' opinions, which might not be your opinion.**
7-12. **Not answered.**

## Derived observations (source interpretation)

### Customer Interview #001
- There is evidence of occasional desire for fragrance when the customer does not have it.
- The customer explicitly said the inconvenience was not significant.
- The customer is already a fragrance owner/user and usually keeps 1-2 colognes.
- Sampling appears more compelling to this customer than emergency fragrance access: they said they are more likely to buy after having time with a sampler.
- The club appears more compelling than a generic high-foot-traffic location.
- "Really good scent" appears important to usage.
- Premium/hard-to-find fragrances may be more attractive than ordinary fragrances.
- The customer's "being sober" answer may indicate a nightlife/social-context hypothesis, but this needs testing and is not a customer fact.
- The stated 250kshs price needs clarification because it is unusually specific and may represent a misunderstanding of the question.

### Customer Interview #002
- Problem strength: Stronger signal than Customer #001 because this respondent experienced a real situation where they needed fragrance, used a workaround, and was willing to be late rather than arrive smelling bad. This remains evidence from one respondent, not validation.
- Existing behavior: Strong perfume usage; the respondent says they always have perfume on.
- Sampling: Confirmed interest.
- Concept interest: Positive, but the respondent had already seen something similar with a friend during a night out, so this is not independent proof of demand.
- Venue signal: Gym and nightclubs.
- Value proposition signal: Freshness plus convenience/proximity.
- Fragrance concern: Poor scent selection or incompatible combinations could reduce use.
- WTP: The respondent stated "Splash at 100 KShs." Record this as CUSTOMER EVIDENCE / UNVALIDATED, not validated pricing.
- Assortment signal: The respondent favors following market trends / perfume-shop selection, with convenience being the differentiator.
- Overall evidence strength: TWO CUSTOMERS - still insufficient to validate the business.

### Customer Interview #003
- Customer 3 does not validate the core problem of needing fragrance while away from home.
- Deodorant is an alternative behavior for this customer if the situation occurs.
- Occasion-driven fragrance purchasing is supported by this interview.
- Sampling is a strong behavior signal: the customer always tests first, including fragrances they have used before.
- No inference can be made yet about willingness to use, pay for, or seek out a fragrance vending machine because Q7-Q12 were not answered.
- This evidence should remain separate from the conclusions drawn from Customers 1 and 2.

### Apogee Interaction
* **Command:** "I want to know what we’ve learned so far from the people I interviewed about the perfume vending idea. * **Action:** "Here's the honest state of play from the six interviews so far.

## What customers actually said

**On the core problem (finding themselves without fragrance when they need it):**
- 4 of 6 customers said yes, this has happened to them (#001, #002, #004, #005)
- 2 of 6 said no, it hasn't (#003, #006)
- Of the "yes" group, the severity varies a lot — #001 and #004 called it a minor inconvenience, while #002 and #005 described real friction: #002 was willing to be late to a meeting rather than show up smelling bad, and #005 spent money and time driving around looking for a substitute and ended up stuck with a scent they didn't like.

**On sampling before buying:**
- This is the most consistent signal across the board. Every customer who answered this question (#001, #003, #004, #006 explicitly, plus #002 and #005 implicitly) wants to try a fragrance before committing to a full bottle. #005 went further, calling it "the biggest issue with scents" — you have to smell it in person, and nothing online solves that.

**On occasion-driven buying:**
- Mixed. #001 and #002 said they don't buy for specific occasions — they just like to always have a signature scent on hand. #003, #004, #005, and #006 said they do buy for occasions.

**On venue, pricing, and vending-machine interest specifically:**
- Only two customers (#001 and #002) answered these questions at all. #003 gave partial venue answers. #004, #005, and #006 didn't answer questions 7–12 at all.
- Where answered, venue suggestions varied: club/university crowd (#001), gym/nightclub (#002), barbershop/gym/offices/high-end bathrooms/nightclub/airport (#003).
- Pricing answers were inconsistent and possibly confused: #001 said 250 KSh per spray, #002 said 100 KSh per splash, #003 wanted to calculate based on total ounce price. These aren't comparable numbers yet — we don't even know if "spray" and "splash" mean the same thing to different people.

## What looks like a real pattern

- **Sampling/try-before-buy is the strongest and most consistent signal in the data.** Multiple customers raised it independently, without being led there, and one called it the single biggest problem with buying fragrance at all.
- **The emergency-need problem is real for some but not universal.** Two-thirds of respondents have experienced it, but its severity ranges from "no big deal" to "changed my behavior and cost me money."

## What people disagreed on

- Whether being without fragrance is actually a problem worth solving (4 yes vs 2 no)
- Whether fragrance purchases are occasion-driven or just ongoing personal habit
- Where a vending machine would make sense (club, gym, nightclub, barbershop, office, airport — no consensus, and mostly single-customer opinions)
- What a reasonable price is (numbers don't line up, and units of measurement — spray vs. splash vs. ounce — aren't standardized)

## What we still don't know

This is the bigger issue: **questions 7 through 12 — the ones that actually test the vending machine concept, venue fit, usage triggers, and pricing — are unanswered for half the interviews** (#004, #005, #006), and only partially answered for #003. So we have:
- No tested data on whether people would actually use a vending machine (as opposed to just liking the idea in the abstract)
- No reliable pricing signal — what exists is contradictory and thin
- No venue consensus
- No fragrance assortment consensus beyond vague "premium" or "long-lasting" preferences

**Bottom line:** six interviews is not enough to validate anything, and the interviews themselves are inconsistently completed — the questions that matter most for a go/no-go on the vending concept are the ones most often skipped. The one thing that does look solid is the demand for sampling before purchase; that's worth probing deeper and explicitly in every remaining interview, along with making sure Q7–Q12 actually get asked every time."

* **Command:** "What have we learned so far from the customer interviews about the perfume vending idea? Tell me the strongest patterns, where customers differed, and what we still don't know. Base your answer only on the interviews we've actually completed.”"
* **Action:** "Here's the honest state of play across all 6 completed interviews:

**Strongest pattern: sampling matters, a lot**
Every single customer who answered that question (5 of 6 — #1, #2, #3, #4, #6) said they want to try a fragrance before buying the whole bottle. This is the most consistent finding across the whole interview set, no disagreement. Customer #5 went further and framed it as an unsolved problem: "you have to smell it before buying it, and technology hasn't solved that for online shopping." Sampling looks like the closest thing to a validated pattern you have — but note, none of that is the same as validating that people would pay to sample from a machine, since Q7-12 (the vending-specific questions) went unanswered for #4, #5, and #6.

**Second pattern, weaker and split: the "unexpectedly need fragrance" problem**
- Support: #1, #2, #4, #5 all said yes, they've been caught without fragrance.
- No support: #3 and #6 said no.
- Severity varies widely. #2 and #5 described real cost (lateness, wasted money, wrong scent as a compromise) — these are your strongest problem signals. #1 and #4 both explicitly said the inconvenience was "not much" / "not particularly big."

So this isn't a uniform pain point — it's real for some, mild-to-nonexistent for others. That's a genuine disagreement in the data, not just noise.

**Occasion-driven purchasing**
Mixed and inconsistent. #3, #5, and #6 said they buy perfume mainly for a particular occasion. #1 and #2 said no — they wear it as a habitual thing, not occasion-tied (#1: "I usually have 1-2 colognes at a time... one longtime favourite"; #2: "always have perfume on anytime"). No clear consensus here.

**Venue/context — where the two people who answered it disagreed on framing but converged on nightlife**
Only #1, #2, and #3 answered the venue question. #1 said club/university-student environments specifically ("most men already know how they want to smell" — sober use is a barrier for him). #2 said gym and nightclubs. #3 said barbershops, gyms, offices, airports — a broader convenience-location theory, but also flagged "seems costly to manage." So nightlife/gym shows up twice, but it's far from consensus, and #3's answer pulls in a completely different direction (everyday convenience locations rather than social/nightlife).

**Pricing — do not treat this as settled**
Two data points, wildly different: #1 said ~250 KSh per spray (flagged in the source as needing clarification — unclear if that's really per-spray), #2 said 100 KSh for a "splash" (also undefined). #3 wants proportional per-ounce pricing. That's three different mental models for pricing in three answers. No usable number yet.

**What we genuinely don't know**
- Willingness to actually use or pay for a *vending machine specifically* — untested for #3, #4, #5, #6 (only #1 and #2 got that far).
- Whether nightlife/social context is a real driver or just two people's suggestion.
- What "a spray" or "a splash" even means to customers in volume/price terms.
- Whether premium/hard-to-find fragrance assortment (only mentioned by #1) is broadly desired.
- Fragrance preferences in general — mostly unanswered.

**Bottom line:** With 6 interviews, half of them incomplete on the vending-specific questions, this remains insufficient evidence to validate the business — the source material is explicit about that at every single interview. The sampling behavior is the one thing that shows up consistently and could be worth designing the next round of interviews around. Everything else — the emergency-problem severity, venue, occasion vs. habitual use, and pricing — is either split or too thin to draw a conclusion from yet."

**What we should assume about spray volume:** Nothing. There's no operational definition (ml, number of pumps, seconds of dispense) attached to either term in any of the six interviews. **Does this change our pricing evidence?** Yes, in an important way: it means the two price points we have (250 KSh and 100 KSh) are **not directly comparable**. They could be:
- Two different prices for the same unit (real WTP variance), or
- Two different units being priced (a "spray" ≠ a "splash"), which would make the discrepancy an artifact of terminology, not a pricing signal at all.

Right now the vault correctly logs both as "CUSTOMER EVIDENCE / UNVALIDATED," and that status still holds — but the underlying reason for the gap between them is now clearer: we don't have a shared definition of what's being purchased. So this isn't just "two data points, need more" — it's "two data points measuring possibly different things, need a shared unit before they're even comparable."

**Practical implication:** Any future interview should pin down the unit explicitly — e.g., ask "how many sprays/ml for that price?" — before we can treat spray-volume pricing as real evidence rather than two anecdotes that might not be talking about the same product."

### Customer Interview #001

	- Customer explanation: "Yes I am more likely to buy if I have a sampler that I have time to get to like."

	- Customer suggested: "At the club or at a place where many university students go."

## Derived observations (source interpretation)

### Customer Interview #001
- There is evidence of occasional desire for fragrance when the customer does not have it.
- The customer explicitly said the inconvenience was not significant.
- The customer is already a fragrance owner/user and usually keeps 1-2 colognes.
- Sampling appears more compelling to this customer than emergency fragrance access: they said they are more likely to buy after having time with a sampler.
- The club appears more compelling than a generic high-foot-traffic location.
- "Really good scent" appears important to usage.
- Premium/hard-to-find fragrances may be more attractive than ordinary fragrances.
- The customer's "being sober" answer may indicate a nightlife/social-context hypothesis, but this needs testing and is not a customer fact.
- The stated 250kshs price needs clarification because it is unusually specific and may represent a misunderstanding of the question.

### Customer Interview #002
- Problem strength: Stronger signal than Customer #001 because this respondent experienced a real situation where they needed fragrance, used a workaround, and was willing to be late rather than arrive smelling bad. This remains evidence from one respondent, not validation.
- Existing behavior: Strong perfume usage; the respondent says they always have perfume on.
- Sampling: Confirmed interest.
- Concept interest: Positive, but the respondent had already seen something similar with a friend during a night out, so this is not independent proof of demand.
- Venue signal: Gym and nightclubs.
- Value proposition signal: Freshness plus convenience/proximity.
- Fragrance concern: Poor scent selection or incompatible combinations could reduce use.
- WTP: The respondent stated "Splash at 100 KShs." Record this as CUSTOMER EVIDENCE / UNVALIDATED, not validated pricing.
- Assortment signal: The respondent favors following market trends / perfume-shop selection, with convenience being the differentiator.
- Overall evidence strength: TWO CUSTOMERS - still insufficient to validate the business.

### Customer Interview #003
- Customer 3 does not validate the core problem of needing fragrance while away from home.
- Deodorant is an alternative behavior for this customer if the situation occurs.
- Occasion-driven fragrance purchasing is supported by this interview.
- Sampling is a strong behavior signal: the customer always tests first, including fragrances they have used before.
- No inference can be made yet about willingness to use, pay for, or seek out a fragrance vending machine because Q7-Q12 were not answered.
- This evidence should remain separate from the conclusions drawn from Customers 1 and 2.

### Customer Interview #004
- The customer reports a situation where they wanted to be properly presented after an exhausting day.
- The inconvenience was described as not particularly significant, although the feeling of not being at their best was difficult to shake.
- The customer reports occasion-related fragrance use.
- Fragrance choice appears strongly driven by personal scent preference rather than brand or quantity.
- No evidence was collected from Q7-Q12 about vending-machine interest, venue, usage conditions, pricing, or assortment.

### Customer Interview #005
- This customer provides a clear real-world signal of an unmet fragrance-access problem while already away from home.
- The workaround created both monetary and time inconvenience and forced a compromise on scent choice.
- The customer's comments indicate that personal scent preference is important and that an unsuitable substitute is itself a problem.
- Occasion-driven fragrance use is supported by this interview.
- The "aura farm" explanation suggests scent is viewed as part of social presentation and identity; this remains one customer's language, not a validated market pattern.
- Sampling is described as a major unresolved problem because the customer wants to smell a fragrance personally before purchasing.
- Q7-Q12 were not answered, so vending-machine interest, venue, pricing, and operational preferences remain untested.

### Customer Interview #006
- Customer 6 does not support the core problem of unexpectedly needing fragrance while away from home.
- The customer does report occasion-related fragrance purchasing.
- The customer reports wanting to try a fragrance before buying the whole bottle.
- No evidence was collected from Q7-Q12 about vending-machine interest, venue, usage conditions, pricing, or assortment.

## Open follow-up questions (not findings)

### Follow-up questions from Customer Interview #001
1. Do other customers also say the club is the strongest use case?
2. Is the real use case emergency fragrance, nightlife preparation, or fragrance discovery/sampling?
3. Do customers actually consider the lack of perfume a meaningful problem?
4. Would people pay for a spray when sober, or is alcohol/social context important?
5. What exactly does the customer mean by KSh 250 per spray?
6. Are premium/hard-to-find fragrances genuinely more attractive to customers?
7. Do university students represent a particularly strong customer segment?
8. Would customers rather pay for a spray or receive a sample that can be taken away?
9. What fragrances do customers actually want when asked without prompting?

### Follow-up questions from Customer Interview #002
1. Does the emergency/fragrance problem recur for other customers?
2. Is convenience/proximity consistently more important than the vending-machine format?
3. Do gym and nightclub users behave differently?
4. Is KSh 100 consistently acceptable for a splash?
5. What exactly counts as a "splash" to customers?
6. Which fragrances do customers actually want?
7. Does the respondent's prior exposure to a similar machine indicate an existing competitor or simply an isolated example?

## Researcher clarifications and supporting market research

## Researcher Clarifications and Supporting Market Research

> These notes are researcher interpretation/supporting research. They are NOT customer evidence and must not be presented as customer statements or validation findings.

### Terminology clarification: spray vs. splash

For analysis, treat a customer saying **"spray"** and a customer saying **"splash"** as the same unit: one perfume spray/dispense. Do not treat "splash" as a separate volume unit.

### Working spray-volume assumption

Supporting market research indicates that a conventional fine-fragrance atomizer commonly dispenses approximately **0.10 mL per spray**. A practical working range is approximately **0.08�0.12 mL per spray**, although actual pumps vary.

Treat 0.10 mL as a **working research assumption, not customer evidence and not a universal standard**. Any eventual vending hardware must be measured or specified separately.

### Interpretation rules for current validation

- Customer #001's KSh 250 per spray and Customer #002's KSh 100 per splash may be compared as observations of **price per spray/dispense**, because "spray" and "splash" are being treated as the same unit.
- These prices remain **unvalidated customer price observations**, not established market pricing or a validated willingness-to-pay point.
- Do not treat vending-machine willingness as resolved; it remains open pending further evidence.
- Do not treat nightlife/social context as resolved; it remains open pending further evidence.
- Premium/hard-to-find assortment and fragrance preferences should be determined through follow-up questions, not inferred as findings.

### Evidence handling rule

Keep researcher clarification and supporting market research separate from raw customer evidence. Never present researcher assumptions as customer statements.

### Source for spray-volume reference

Working reference based on current supporting market research: common fragrance atomizer outputs are approximately 0.08�0.12 mL per actuation, with 0.10 mL used as the practical planning assumption. This is supporting market research only and does not constitute validation of the perfume-vending idea."

- Overall evidence strength: TWO CUSTOMERS - still insufficient to validate the business.

### Customer Interview #003
- Customer 3 does not validate the core problem of needing fragrance while away from home.
- Deodorant is an alternative behavior for this customer if the situation occurs.
- Occasion-driven fragrance purchasing is supported by this interview.
- Sampling is a strong behavior signal: the customer always tests first, including fragrances they have used before.
- No inference can be made yet about willingness to use, pay for, or seek out a fragrance vending machine because Q7-Q12 were not answered.
- This evidence should remain separate from the conclusions drawn from Customers 1 and 2.

### Customer Interview #004
- The customer reports a situation where they wanted to be properly presented after an exhausting day.
- The inconvenience was described as not particularly significant, although the feeling of not being at their best was difficult to shake.
- The customer reports occasion-related fragrance use.
- Fragrance choice appears strongly driven by personal scent preference rather than brand or quantity.
- No evidence was collected from Q7-Q12 about vending-machine interest, venue, usage conditions, pricing, or assortment.

### Customer Interview #005
- This customer provides a clear real-world signal of an unmet fragrance-access problem while already away from home.
- The workaround created both monetary and time inconvenience and forced a compromise on scent choice.
- The customer's comments indicate that personal scent preference is important and that an unsuitable substitute is itself a problem.
- Occasion-driven fragrance use is supported by this interview.
- The "aura farm" explanation suggests scent is viewed as part of social presentation and identity; this remains one customer's language, not a validated market pattern.
- Sampling is described as a major unresolved problem because the customer wants to smell a fragrance personally before purchasing.
- Q7-Q12 were not answered, so vending-machine interest, venue, pricing, and operational preferences remain untested.

### Customer Interview #006
- Customer 6 does not support the core problem of unexpectedly needing fragrance while away from home.
- The customer does report occasion-related fragrance purchasing.
- The customer reports wanting to try a fragrance before buying the whole bottle.
- No evidence was collected from Q7-Q12 about vending-machine interest, venue, usage conditions, pricing, or assortment.

## Open follow-up questions (not findings)

### Follow-up questions from Customer Interview #001
1. Do other customers also say the club is the strongest use case?
2. Is the real use case emergency fragrance, nightlife preparation, or fragrance discovery/sampling?
3. Do customers actually consider the lack of perfume a meaningful problem?
4. Would people pay for a spray when sober, or is alcohol/social context important?
5. What exactly does the customer mean by KSh 250 per spray?
6. Are premium/hard-to-find fragrances genuinely more attractive to customers?
7. Do university students represent a particularly strong customer segment?
8. Would customers rather pay for a spray or receive a sample that can be taken away?
9. What fragrances do customers actually want when asked without prompting?

### Follow-up questions from Customer Interview #002
1. Does the emergency/fragrance problem recur for other customers?
2. Is convenience/proximity consistently more important than the vending-machine format?
3. Do gym and nightclub users behave differently?
4. Is KSh 100 consistently acceptable for a splash?
5. What exactly counts as a "splash" to customers?
6. Which fragrances do customers actually want?
7. Does the respondent's prior exposure to a similar machine indicate an existing competitor or simply an isolated example?

## Researcher clarifications and supporting market research

## Researcher Clarifications and Supporting Market Research

> These notes are researcher interpretation/supporting research. They are NOT customer evidence and must not be presented as customer statements or validation findings.

### Terminology clarification: spray vs. splash

For analysis, treat a customer saying **"spray"** and a customer saying **"splash"** as the same unit: one perfume spray/dispense. Do not treat "splash" as a separate volume unit.

### Working spray-volume assumption

Supporting market research indicates that a conventional fine-fragrance atomizer commonly dispenses approximately **0.10 mL per spray**. A practical working range is approximately **0.08�0.12 mL per spray**, although actual pumps vary.

Treat 0.10 mL as a **working research assumption, not customer evidence and not a universal standard**. Any eventual vending hardware must be measured or specified separately.

### Interpretation rules for current validation

- Customer #001's KSh 250 per spray and Customer #002's KSh 100 per splash may be compared as observations of **price per spray/dispense**, because "spray" and "splash" are being treated as the same unit.
- These prices remain **unvalidated customer price observations**, not established market pricing or a validated willingness-to-pay point.
- Do not treat vending-machine willingness as resolved; it remains open pending further evidence.
- Do not treat nightlife/social context as resolved; it remains open pending further evidence.
- Premium/hard-to-find assortment and fragrance preferences should be determined through follow-up questions, not inferred as findings.

### Evidence handling rule

Keep researcher clarification and supporting market research separate from raw customer evidence. Never present researcher assumptions as customer statements.

### Source for spray-volume reference

Working reference based on current supporting market research: common fragrance atomizer outputs are approximately 0.08�0.12 mL per actuation, with 0.10 mL used as the practical planning assumption. This is supporting market research only and does not constitute validation of the perfume-vending idea."

**Conversation history vs. vault context**

Conversation history — what you see in `interaction_history.json` and the Master_Note interaction logs — is basically a transcript. It records *that* an exchange happened: the query, a short label for what mode I was in, a status score. It's chronological and largely unstructured. It's useful as an audit trail ("when did the Claude API start failing," "how many times did Dan ask if I was running"), but it's not designed to answer questions. Nothing in it is indexed by topic, and old entries don't get reconciled with new ones — they just pile up. You can see that clearly in what's in front of me right now: five nearly identical "Apogee is configured for Claude-first..." facts, timestamped minutes apart, because each confirmation got logged as a new fact rather than updating one persistent record.

Vault context — things like the Active Focus dashboard, the Perfume Vending Validation project note, the Legal_and_iTax folder — is structured, current-state material. It's meant to be retrieved and reasoned over: it has status fields, known/unknown breakdowns, checkboxes, links. This is the stuff that should be authoritative for "what's true right now."

The problem you're pointing at is that a builder agent roadmap — if it only ever gets discussed in conversation and logged as interaction history — lives in the wrong bucket. It becomes a sequence of chat turns and status-check log entries, not a retrievable document with sections, current status, and next steps. So when you ask me to pull up "the roadmap," I have no single structured object to retrieve — only scattered mentions across interaction logs, none of which are indexed as "this is the roadmap."

**What I think needs fixing**

1. **No canonical roadmap note exists.** There's no vault file analogous to the Perfume Vending Validation note — with Status / Known / Unknown / Next Step fields — for the builder agent work. Until one exists, there's nothing for me to reliably retrieve, no matter how many times it's discussed.

2. **Interaction history is being used as a memory substitute.** The repeated Claude-first/Ollama-fallback fact is a symptom: the system is writing runtime status into `apogee_memory.json` as a new timestamped "fact" every time it's confirmed, rather than updating one persistent status record. If the roadmap gets treated the same way — restated in conversation and logged as an interaction — it'll suffer the same fate: many redundant mentions, no single source of truth.

3. **No update discipline distinguishing "new fact" from "status update."** For something like a roadmap that evolves (steps get completed, priorities shift), the vault needs a single note that gets *edited* in place — checkboxes flipped, status field changed — not a growing pile of dated snapshots.

The fix isn't something I can do from here — it's a structural one: create a dedicated roadmap note under the Active Engine (or wherever it logically sits), give it the same Known/Unknown/Next-Step shape as your other active projects, and make sure updates to it are edits to that note rather than new conversation log entries. Once that note exists in the vault, I'll retrieve it reliably every time, the same way I do with the Perfume Vending project right now.

## Derived observations (source interpretation)

### Customer Interview #001
- There is evidence of occasional desire for fragrance when the customer does not have it.
- The customer explicitly said the inconvenience was not significant.
- The customer is already a fragrance owner/user and usually keeps 1-2 colognes.
- Sampling appears more compelling to this customer than emergency fragrance access: they said they are more likely to buy after having time with a sampler.
- The club appears more compelling than a generic high-foot-traffic location.
- "Really good scent" appears important to usage.
- Premium/hard-to-find fragrances may be more attractive than ordinary fragrances.
- The customer's "being sober" answer may indicate a nightlife/social-context hypothesis, but this needs testing and is not a customer fact.
- The stated 250kshs price needs clarification because it is unusually specific and may represent a misunderstanding of the question.

### Customer Interview #002
- Problem strength: Stronger signal than Customer #001 because this respondent experienced a real situation where they needed fragrance, used a workaround, and was willing to be late rather than arrive smelling bad. This remains evidence from one respondent, not validation.
- Existing behavior: Strong perfume usage; the respondent says they always have perfume on.
- Sampling: Confirmed interest.
- Concept interest: Positive, but the respondent had already seen something similar with a friend during a night out, so this is not independent proof of demand.
- Venue signal: Gym and nightclubs.
- Value proposition signal: Freshness plus convenience/proximity.
- Fragrance concern: Poor scent selection or incompatible combinations could reduce use.
- WTP: The respondent stated "Splash at 100 KShs." Record this as CUSTOMER EVIDENCE / UNVALIDATED, not validated pricing.
- Assortment signal: The respondent favors following market trends / perfume-shop selection, with convenience being the differentiator.
- Overall evidence strength: TWO CUSTOMERS - still insufficient to validate the business.

### Customer Interview #003
- Customer 3 does not validate the core problem of needing fragrance while away from home.
- Deodorant is an alternative behavior for this customer if the situation occurs.
- Occasion-driven fragrance purchasing is supported by this interview.
- Sampling is a strong behavior signal: the customer always tests first, including fragrances they have used before.
- No inference can be made yet about willingness to use, pay for, or seek out a fragrance vending machine because Q7-Q12 were not answered.
- This evidence should remain separate from the conclusions drawn from Customers 1 and 2.

### Customer Interview #004
- The customer reports a situation where they wanted to be properly presented after an exhausting day.
- The inconvenience was described as not particularly significant, although the feeling of not being at their best was difficult to shake.
- The customer reports occasion-related fragrance use.
- Fragrance choice appears strongly driven by personal scent preference rather than brand or quantity.
- No evidence was collected from Q7-Q12 about vending-machine interest, venue, usage conditions, pricing, or assortment.

### Customer Interview #005
- This customer provides a clear real-world signal of an unmet fragrance-access problem while already away from home.
- The workaround created both monetary and time inconvenience and forced a compromise on scent choice.
- The customer's comments indicate that personal scent preference is important and that an unsuitable substitute is itself a problem.
- Occasion-driven fragrance use is supported by this interview.
- The "aura farm" explanation suggests scent is viewed as part of social presentation and identity; this remains one customer's language, not a validated market pattern.
- Sampling is described as a major unresolved problem because the customer wants to smell a fragrance personally before purchasing.
- Q7-Q12 were not answered, so vending-machine interest, venue, pricing, and operational preferences remain untested.

### Customer Interview #006
- Customer 6 does not support the core problem of unexpectedly needing fragrance while away from home.
- The customer does report occasion-related fragrance purchasing.
- The customer reports wanting to try a fragrance before buying the whole bottle.
- No evidence was collected from Q7-Q12 about vending-machine interest, venue, usage conditions, pricing, or assortment.

### Follow-up questions from Customer Interview #001
1. Do other customers also say the club is the strongest use case?
2. Is the real use case emergency fragrance, nightlife preparation, or fragrance discovery/sampling?
3. Do customers actually consider the lack of perfume a meaningful problem?
4. Would people pay for a spray when sober, or is alcohol/social context important?
5. What exactly does the customer mean by KSh 250 per spray?
6. Are premium/hard-to-find fragrances genuinely more attractive to customers?
7. Do university students represent a particularly strong customer segment?
8. Would customers rather pay for a spray or receive a sample that can be taken away?
9. What fragrances do customers actually want when asked without prompting?

### Follow-up questions from Customer Interview #002

2. Is convenience/proximity consistently more important than the vending-machine format?
3. Do gym and nightclub users behave differently?

## Researcher clarifications and supporting market research

## Researcher Clarifications and Supporting Market Research

> These notes are researcher interpretation/supporting research. They are NOT customer evidence and must not be presented as customer statements or validation findings.

### Terminology clarification: spray vs. splash

For analysis, treat a customer saying **"spray"** and a customer saying **"splash"** as the same unit: one perfume spray/dispense. Do not treat "splash" as a separate volume unit.

### Working spray-volume assumption

Supporting market research indicates that a conventional fine-fragrance atomizer commonly dispenses approximately **0.10 mL per spray**. A practical working range is approximately **0.08�0.12 mL per spray**, although actual pumps vary.

Treat 0.10 mL as a **working research assumption, not customer evidence and not a universal standard**. Any eventual vending hardware must be measured or specified separately.

### Interpretation rules for current validation

- Customer #001's KSh 250 per spray and Customer #002's KSh 100 per splash may be compared as observations of **price per spray/dispense**, because "spray" and "splash" are being treated as the same unit.
- These prices remain **unvalidated customer price observations**, not established market pricing or a validated willingness-to-pay point.
- Do not treat vending-machine willingness as resolved; it remains open pending further evidence.
- Do not treat nightlife/social context as resolved; it remains open pending further evidence.
- Premium/hard-to-find assortment and fragrance preferences should be determined through follow-up questions, not inferred as findings.

### Evidence handling rule

Keep researcher clarification and supporting market research separate from raw customer evidence. Never present researcher assumptions as customer statements.

### Source for spray-volume reference

Working reference based on current supporting market research: common fragrance atomizer outputs are approximately 0.08�0.12 mL per actuation, with 0.10 mL used as the practical planning assumption. This is supporting market research only and does not constitute validation of the perfume-vending idea."

### Customer Interview #001
- There is evidence of occasional desire for fragrance when the customer does not have it.
- The customer explicitly said the inconvenience was not significant.
- The customer is already a fragrance owner/user and usually keeps 1-2 colognes.
- Sampling appears more compelling to this customer than emergency fragrance access: they said they are more likely to buy after having time with a sampler.
- The club appears more compelling than a generic high-foot-traffic location.
- "Really good scent" appears important to usage.
- Premium/hard-to-find fragrances may be more attractive than ordinary fragrances.
- The customer's "being sober" answer may indicate a nightlife/social-context hypothesis, but this needs testing and is not a customer fact.
- The stated 250kshs price needs clarification because it is unusually specific and may represent a misunderstanding of the question.

### Customer Interview #002
- Problem strength: Stronger signal than Customer #001 because this respondent experienced a real situation where they needed fragrance, used a workaround, and was willing to be late rather than arrive smelling bad. This remains evidence from one respondent, not validation.
- Existing behavior: Strong perfume usage; the respondent says they always have perfume on.
- Sampling: Confirmed interest.
- Concept interest: Positive, but the respondent had already seen something similar with a friend during a night out, so this is not independent proof of demand.
- Venue signal: Gym and nightclubs.
- Value proposition signal: Freshness plus convenience/proximity.
- Fragrance concern: Poor scent selection or incompatible combinations could reduce use.
- WTP: The respondent stated "Splash at 100 KShs." Record this as CUSTOMER EVIDENCE / UNVALIDATED, not validated pricing.
- Assortment signal: The respondent favors following market trends / perfume-shop selection, with convenience being the differentiator.
- Overall evidence strength: TWO CUSTOMERS - still insufficient to validate the business.

### Customer Interview #003
- Customer 3 does not validate the core problem of needing fragrance while away from home.
- Deodorant is an alternative behavior for this customer if the situation occurs.
- Occasion-driven fragrance purchasing is supported by this interview.
- Sampling is a strong behavior signal: the customer always tests first, including fragrances they have used before.
- No inference can be made yet about willingness to use, pay for, or seek out a fragrance vending machine because Q7-Q12 were not answered.
- This evidence should remain separate from the conclusions drawn from Customers 1 and 2.

### Customer Interview #004
- The customer reports a situation where they wanted to be properly presented after an exhausting day.
- The inconvenience was described as not particularly significant, although the feeling of not being at their best was difficult to shake.
- The customer reports occasion-related fragrance use.
- Fragrance choice appears strongly driven by personal scent preference rather than brand or quantity.
- No evidence was collected from Q7-Q12 about vending-machine interest, venue, usage conditions, pricing, or assortment.

### Customer Interview #005
- This customer provides a clear real-world signal of an unmet fragrance-access problem while already away from home.
- The workaround created both monetary and time inconvenience and forced a compromise on scent choice.
- The customer's comments indicate that personal scent preference is important and that an unsuitable substitute is itself a problem.
- Occasion-driven fragrance use is supported by this interview.
- The "aura farm" explanation suggests scent is viewed as part of social presentation and identity; this remains one customer's language, not a validated market pattern.
- Sampling is described as a major unresolved problem because the customer wants to smell a fragrance personally before purchasing.
- Q7-Q12 were not answered, so vending-machine interest, venue, pricing, and operational preferences remain untested.

### Customer Interview #006
- Customer 6 does not support the core problem of unexpectedly needing fragrance while away from home.
- The customer does report occasion-related fragrance purchasing.
- The customer reports wanting to try a fragrance before buying the whole bottle.
- No evidence was collected from Q7-Q12 about vending-machine interest, venue, usage conditions, pricing, or assortment.

## Open follow-up questions (not findings)

### Follow-up questions from Customer Interview #001
1. Do other customers also say the club is the strongest use case?
2. Is the real use case emergency fragrance, nightlife preparation, or fragrance discovery/sampling?
3. Do customers actually consider the lack of perfume a meaningful problem?

7. Do university students represent a particularly strong customer segment?
8. Would customers rather pay for a spray or receive a sample that can be taken away?
9. What fragrances do customers actually want when asked without prompting?

2. Is convenience/proximity consistently more important than the vending-machine format?

## Researcher clarifications and supporting market research

## Researcher Clarifications and Supporting Market Research

> These notes are researcher interpretation/supporting research. They are NOT customer evidence and must not be presented as customer statements or validation findings.

### Terminology clarification: spray vs. splash

For analysis, treat a customer saying **"spray"** and a customer saying **"splash"** as the same unit: one perfume spray/dispense. Do not treat "splash" as a separate volume unit.

### Working spray-volume assumption

Supporting market research indicates that a conventional fine-fragrance atomizer commonly dispenses approximately **0.10 mL per spray**. A practical working range is approximately **0.08�0.12 mL per spray**, although actual pumps vary.

Treat 0.10 mL as a **working research assumption, not customer evidence and not a universal standard**. Any eventual vending hardware must be measured or specified separately.

### Interpretation rules for current validation

- Customer #001's KSh 250 per spray and Customer #002's KSh 100 per splash may be compared as observations of **price per spray/dispense**, because "spray" and "splash" are being treated as the same unit.
- These prices remain **unvalidated customer price observations**, not established market pricing or a validated willingness-to-pay point.
- Do not treat vending-machine willingness as resolved; it remains open pending further evidence.
- Do not treat nightlife/social context as resolved; it remains open pending further evidence.
- Premium/hard-to-find assortment and fragrance preferences should be determined through follow-up questions, not inferred as findings.

### Evidence handling rule

Keep researcher clarification and supporting market research separate from raw customer evidence. Never present researcher assumptions as customer statements.

### Source for spray-volume reference

Working reference based on current supporting market research: common fragrance atomizer outputs are approximately 0.08�0.12 mL per actuation, with 0.10 mL used as the practical planning assumption. This is supporting market research only and does not constitute validation of the perfume-vending idea."

* **Command:** "Explain the perfume vending machine validation so far in detail."
* **Action:** "Here's the full picture on Perfume Vending Validation, based on what's actually documented in your vault:

## Project Status
**TESTING / VALIDATION phase.** This is explicitly bounded as *research only* — no hardware purchase, no GO decision, no "validation" claim has been made yet. The project has a deliberate evidence-discipline system (FACT, OBSERVATION, HYPOTHESIS, ASSUMPTION, CUSTOMER EVIDENCE, MARKET EVIDENCE, SUPPLIER CLAIM, VALIDATED) specifically so that positive signals don't get quietly upgraded into conclusions they don't support.

## The Core Question
Is there real unmet demand for convenient, affordable, trusted perfume-by-the-spray in Kenya — with a venue and operating model that actually works economically?

## What's Established (not evidence of demand, just groundwork)
- A hardware reference point exists: a "P6-style" wall-mounted touchscreen fragrance vending machine, ~5 fragrance slots, payment hardware, individual spray dispensing. **Manufacturer/OEM and exact model are unconfirmed** — "P6-style" is a description, not a verified product identity.
- International and African perfume-vending operators/business models exist. This shows the *concept* exists elsewhere — your vault is explicit that this is **not** evidence the Kenyan opportunity is validated.

## What's Still Unknown (the real gaps)
- Actual Kenyan customer demand
- Kenyan willingness to pay
- Best venue type (gym, nightlife, hotel, barbershop, mall, airport, office — all currently just investigation prompts, not chosen segments)
- Best fragrance assortment
- M-Pesa/payment implementation
- Venue economics
- Exact machine/OEM identity
- Landed hardware cost
- Overall unit economics

## Customer Evidence So Far
Your authoritative interview log shows **8 customer interviews recorded**. I have visibility into partial detail from two of them:

**Customer #001** (partial — questions 1-5 documented):
- Has wished for perfume/fragrance when they didn't have it: **YES**
- What they did instead: used soap under their armpits
- Inconvenience level: **"Not much"**
- Doesn't buy perfume mainly for occasions — buys a scent to "augment myself for a while"

**Customer #4** (answers to questions 7-12 logged):
- Q9 (what would make them use it): no answer captured in what I have
- Q10 (what would stop them from using it): concern about *not being able to try/smell before buying* — worried about uniformity vs. wanting variety
- Q11 (reasonable price for a few sprays): not specified in what's visible to me
- Q12 (expected fragrance types): wants fresh scents; finds fragrance choice "tricky" for someone particular — cares more about how *he* wants to smell than about brand

I do **not** have visibility into interviews #2, #3, #5, #6, #7, or #8, or the remaining answers for #1 and #4 beyond what's shown above. I won't guess at their content.

## Observed Pattern vs. Validated Conclusion
Two data points so far both touch on a "trust/experience before buying" concern — not being sure you'll like a scent, wanting to try before committing. That's a **pattern worth watching**, not a validated finding. Two interviews out of 8 recorded is not enough to call anything confirmed, and I don't have the other six to check whether it holds up or contradicts.

## Next Step (as defined in your framework)
CUSTOMER + VENUE DISCOVERY — continuing structured interviews and starting venue research, using the evidence tables in the Validation Framework note"

## Derived observations (source interpretation)

### Customer Interview #001
- There is evidence of occasional desire for fragrance when the customer does not have it.
- The customer explicitly said the inconvenience was not significant.
- The customer is already a fragrance owner/user and usually keeps 1-2 colognes.
- Sampling appears more compelling to this customer than emergency fragrance access: they said they are more likely to buy after having time with a sampler.
- The club appears more compelling than a generic high-foot-traffic location.
- "Really good scent" appears important to usage.
- Premium/hard-to-find fragrances may be more attractive than ordinary fragrances.
- The customer's "being sober" answer may indicate a nightlife/social-context hypothesis, but this needs testing and is not a customer fact.
- The stated 250kshs price needs clarification because it is unusually specific and may represent a misunderstanding of the question.

### Customer Interview #002
- Problem strength: Stronger signal than Customer #001 because this respondent experienced a real situation where they needed fragrance, used a workaround, and was willing to be late rather than arrive smelling bad. This remains evidence from one respondent, not validation.
- Existing behavior: Strong perfume usage; the respondent says they always have perfume on.
- Sampling: Confirmed interest.
- Concept interest: Positive, but the respondent had already seen something similar with a friend during a night out, so this is not independent proof of demand.
- Venue signal: Gym and nightclubs.
- Value proposition signal: Freshness plus convenience/proximity.
- Fragrance concern: Poor scent selection or incompatible combinations could reduce use.
- WTP: The respondent stated "Splash at 100 KShs." Record this as CUSTOMER EVIDENCE / UNVALIDATED, not validated pricing.
- Assortment signal: The respondent favors following market trends / perfume-shop selection, with convenience being the differentiator.
- Overall evidence strength: TWO CUSTOMERS - still insufficient to validate the business.

### Customer Interview #003
- Customer 3 does not support the core problem of unexpectedly needing fragrance while away from home.
- Deodorant is an alternative behavior for this customer if the situation occurs.
- Occasion-driven fragrance purchasing is supported by this interview.
- Sampling is a strong behavior signal: the customer always tests first, including fragrances they have used before.
- The customer describes the vending-machine idea positively but raises cost/management as a concern.
- The customer identifies "forget" and "top up" as possible use cases.
- Suggested venues include barber shops, gyms, offices, high-end bathrooms, nightclubs, and airports; these remain one-customer venue signals, not validated venue demand.
- No willingness-to-pay amount was provided. The customer's answer indicates that pricing should depend on the underlying fragrance cost and spray economics.
- The customer prefers long-lasting, top-end fragrances.
- This evidence should remain separate from the conclusions drawn from Customers 1 and 2.

### Customer Interview #004
- The customer considers the perfume vending-machine idea excellent, providing direct positive concept feedback.
- Expected locations are gyms, malls, and sports facilities, particularly places where people change clothing, providing a clear venue signal.
- The primary desired outcome is to smell fresh.
- The customer identifies fragrance selection and uniformity as a key barrier: they want confidence that they will like the fragrance and do not want everyone to smell the same.
- The customer gives a stated reasonable price range of Kshs 100 to Kshs 200.
- The customer would prefer deodorant for simply staying fresh and sees fragrance as more difficult because personal scent preference matters more than choosing a brand.
- Overall, the interview provides positive concept interest, concrete venue guidance, a willingness-to-pay signal, and a clear product-design concern around fragrance choice and variety.

### Customer Interview #005
- This customer provides a clear real-world signal of an unmet fragrance-access problem while already away from home.
- The workaround created both monetary and time inconvenience and forced a compromise on scent choice.
- The customer's comments indicate that personal scent preference is important and that an unsuitable substitute is itself a problem.
- Occasion-driven fragrance use is supported by this interview.
- The "aura farm" explanation suggests scent is viewed as part of social presentation and identity; this remains one customer's language, not a validated market pattern.
- Sampling is described as a major unresolved problem because the customer wants to smell a fragrance personally before purchasing.
- Q7-Q12 were not answered, so vending-machine interest, venue, pricing, and operational preferences remain untested.

### Customer Interview #006
- Customer 6 does not support the core problem of unexpectedly needing fragrance while away from home.
- The customer reports a recurring refresh use case: nearly every Friday while in the studio before going out.
- The current workaround is buying deodorant at the supermarket.
- The customer reports occasion-related fragrance purchasing, including for a rugby tournament.
- Sampling is a strong behavior signal: the customer always tests fragrances because some initially smell good but irritate the nose.
- The customer responds very positively to the vending-machine concept.
- Suggested venues include stadiums/events and offices.
- The customer would use the service to freshen up when moving to another venue after a long day.
- The customer stated KSh 50 as a price.
- The customer reported no particular objection and said they mainly want good fragrance.

### Customer Interview #007
- Customer 7 describes a recurring away-from-home fragrance need and already carries an atomizer as a workaround.
- The atomizer solves much of the access problem but limits the customer to one particular scent.
- Layering is an important fragrance behavior for this customer.
- The customer responds positively to the vending-machine concept.
- Nightlife and events are the clearest venue signals, although "all venues" is broader and remains a one-customer signal.
- Variety is a stated requirement; lack of variety could prevent use.
- The customer did not provide a willingness-to-pay amount.
- The customer suggests offering scent profiles that can be layered rather than relying only on specific named fragrances.
- This introduces a potentially useful assortment/product-design hypothesis: flexibility and layering may matter more than a fixed list of individual fragrances for some customers.

### Customer Interview #008
- Customer 8 reports a meaningful desire to smell good but currently cannot afford perfume, indicating a price/access constraint rather than a lack of interest.
- Roll-on deodorant is the current substitute while saving for perfume.
- The customer describes smelling good as an ongoing preference rather than an occasion-specific need.
- Sampling before purchase is supported.
- The customer reacts positively to the concept but believes existing vendors already provide payment channels, so differentiation may be necessary.
- Efficacy, reliability, and convenience are explicit conditions for use.
- No usable willingness-to-pay amount was provided; the respondent says price depends on production cost.
- The customer prefers niche perfumes.
- This interview adds a routine smell-good use case and reinforces the importance of reliability, convenience, sampling, and differentiated assortment.
