## Researcher clarifications and supporting market research

## Researcher Clarifications and Supporting Market Research

> These notes are researcher interpretation/supporting research. They are NOT customer evidence and must not be presented as customer statements or validation findings.

### Terminology clarification: spray vs. splash

For analysis, treat a customer saying **"spray"** and a customer saying **"splash"** as the same unit: one perfume spray/dispense. Do not treat "splash" as a separate volume unit.

### Working spray-volume assumption

Supporting market research indicates that a conventional fine-fragrance atomizer commonly dispenses approximately **0.10 mL per spray**. A practical working range is approximately **0.08–0.12 mL per spray**, although actual pumps vary.

Treat 0.10 mL as a **working research assumption, not customer evidence and not a universal standard**. Any eventual vending hardware must be measured or specified separately.

### Interpretation rules for current validation

- Customer #001's KSh 250 per spray and Customer #002's KSh 100 per splash may be compared as observations of **price per spray/dispense**, because "spray" and "splash" are being treated as the same unit.
- These prices remain **unvalidated customer price observations**, not established market pricing or a validated willingness-to-pay point.
- Do not treat vending-machine willingness as resolved; it remains open pending further evidence.
- Do not treat nightlife/social context as resolved; it remains open pending further evidence.
- Premium/hard-to-find assortment and fragrance preferences should be determined through follow-up questions, not inferred as findings.

### Evidence handling rule

Keep researcher clarification and supporting market research separate from raw customer evidence. Never present researcher assumptions as customer statements.

### Source for spray-volume reference

Working reference based on current supporting market research: common fragrance atomizer outputs are approximately 0.08–0.12 mL per actuation, with 0.10 mL used as the practical planning assumption. This is supporting market research only and does not constitute validation of the perfume-vending idea."

* **Command:** "Read only the section titled Remaining Research Questions After Customer Interview #008 from Validation_Framework.md and reproduce only that section. Do not read or use any other file or section. Do not modify files."
* **Action:** "### Remaining Research Questions After Customer Interview #008

#### Customer problem and use case
1. Which use case is strongest across customers: emergency access, top-up/refresh, sampling/discovery, or occasion preparation?
2. How frequently does the relevant use case actually occur?
3. Do customers consider the problem important enough to spend money solving?
4. What alternatives do customers currently use, and what do those alternatives fail to provide?
5. Does the use case remain relevant outside nightlife and other social situations?

#### Customer segment and venue
6. Which customer segment shows the strongest repeated behavior rather than simply positive concept feedback?
7. Which venue types have the right combination of customer need, traffic, dwell time, and convenience?
8. Would venue owners/managers actually agree to host the machine?
9. What venue economics would make hosting worthwhile?
10. Does the best venue differ depending on the customer use case?

#### Pricing and product
11. What price per dispense is acceptable when tested against a real or clearly defined product offering?
12. What exactly should one dispense provide in volume and user experience?
13. Does fragrance variety materially affect willingness to use the machine?
14. How important are premium, niche, long-lasting, or layerable fragrances?
15. Do customers prefer named fragrances, scent profiles, or a combination?
16. Is sampling a stronger reason to use the machine than emergency access or refresh?

#### Payment and operations
17. What payment flow creates the least friction for customers in Kenyan venues?
18. What reliability and maintenance standard would customers and venues expect?
19. How often would replenishment, cleaning, servicing, or other intervention be required?
20. What operating responsibilities would belong to Apogee versus the venue?

#### Commercial viability
21. Can realistic fragrance, packaging, payment, venue, maintenance, and hardware costs support the observed price range?
22. What minimum usage level would be required for viable unit economics?
23. Is demand recurring enough to distinguish a real business use case from novelty?
24. What evidence would justify moving from customer/venue discovery to a controlled pilot?"

### Apogee Interaction
* **Command:** "What are the main unknowns in the perfume vending validation project?"
* **Action:** "The main unknowns in the perfume vending validation project are:

1. **Kenyan customer demand** — whether people actually want this
2. **Willingness to pay** — whether Kenyan customers would pay for perfume-by-the-spray, and at what price
3. **Best venue** — where this should be placed (gym, mall, nightlife, hotel, barbershop, airport, office — all currently just investigation prompts, not selected)
4. **Best fragrance assortment** — which scents to stock
5. **Payment implementation** — specifically M-Pesa integration
6. **Venue economics** — whether the business model works financially for whoever hosts the machine
7. **Exact machine/OEM** — the "P6-style" hardware is just a description; the actual manufacturer and model are unconfirmed
8. **Landed hardware cost** — what it would actually cost to get a machine into Kenya
9. **Unit economics** — whether the overall numbers work

What *is* known at this point is limited to: a P6-style hardware reference has been identified, and international/African examples of perfume vending operators exist. That's evidence the concept exists elsewhere — it is explicitly **not** evidence that Kenyan demand is real.

The project is still in RESEARCH/VALIDATION phase, and the next step defined for it is customer + venue discovery — i.e., actual interviews and venue investigation, not further desk research. Worth noting: no hardware purchase, GO decision, or validation claim has been made yet."

### Evidence status

The eight customer interviews provide useful signals around access/top-up needs, refresh use, sampling, convenience, fragrance choice, venue types, and price observations. These remain **customer evidence from a small sample**, not validation.

Price observations currently include KSh 50, KSh 100, KSh 100–200, and KSh 250, but there is not yet enough evidence to establish a validated willingness-to-pay point.

Venue suggestions include gyms, malls, sports facilities, offices, stadiums/events, nightlife venues, airports, barber shops, and other high-traffic or changing environments. These are venue hypotheses, not venue validation.

The strongest recurring signal so far is interest in **fragrance sampling/discovery**, while evidence for the underlying "I unexpectedly need perfume while away from home" problem is mixed across customers.

No GO/NO-GO conclusion should be drawn from the current eight interviews.

### Remaining Research Questions After Customer Interview #008

#### Customer problem and use case
1. Which use case is strongest across customers: emergency access, top-up/refresh, sampling/discovery, or occasion preparation?
2. How frequently does the relevant use case actually occur?
3. Do customers consider the problem important enough to spend money solving?
4. What alternatives do customers currently use, and what do those alternatives fail to provide?
5. Does the use case remain relevant outside nightlife and other social situations?

#### Customer segment and venue
6. Which customer segment shows the strongest repeated behavior rather than simply positive concept feedback?
7. Which venue types have the right combination of customer need, traffic, dwell time, and convenience?
8. Would venue owners/managers actually agree to host the machine?
9. What venue economics would make hosting worthwhile?
10. Does the best venue differ depending on the customer use case?

#### Pricing and product
11. What price per dispense is acceptable when tested against a real or clearly defined product offering?
12. What exactly should one dispense provide in volume and user experience?
13. Does fragrance variety materially affect willingness to use the machine?
14. How important are premium, niche, long-lasting, or layerable fragrances?
15. Do customers prefer named fragrances, scent profiles, or a combination?
16. Is sampling a stronger reason to use the machine than emergency access or refresh?

#### Payment and operations
17. What payment flow creates the least friction for customers in Kenyan venues?
18. What reliability and maintenance standard would customers and venues expect?
19. How often would replenishment, cleaning, servicing, or other intervention be required?
20. What operating responsibilities would belong to Apogee versus the venue?

#### Commercial viability
21. Can realistic fragrance, packaging, payment, venue, maintenance, and hardware costs support the observed price range?
22. What minimum usage level would be required for viable unit economics?
23. Is demand recurring enough to distinguish a real business use case from novelty?
24. What evidence would justify moving from customer/venue discovery to a controlled pilot?

### Evidence status

The eight customer interviews provide useful signals around access/top-up needs, refresh use, sampling, convenience, fragrance choice, venue types, and price observations. These remain **customer evidence from a small sample**, not validation.

Price observations currently include KSh 50, KSh 100, KSh 100–200, and KSh 250, but there is not yet enough evidence to establish a validated willingness-to-pay point.

Venue suggestions include gyms, malls, sports facilities, offices, stadiums/events, nightlife venues, airports, barber shops, and other high-traffic or changing environments. These are venue hypotheses, not venue validation.

The strongest recurring signal so far is interest in **fragrance sampling/discovery**, while evidence for the underlying "I unexpectedly need perfume while away from home" problem is mixed across customers.

No GO/NO-GO conclusion should be drawn from the current eight interviews.

The external research was not performed. Handoff created: HO-1789729310271-um5xb2"

### Apogee Interaction
* **Command:** "What is Apogee?"
* **Action:** "Apogee is the personal intelligence system you're building — essentially a second brain and "Life CEO" for Dan and Apogee SKOPE LLP.

The core idea: it's meant to combine your experience, knowledge, goals, and current situation to help you reason better and make better decisions — not just store notes or answer questions like a generic chatbot. It understands natural language, retrieves the right context from your vault, and (within defined permissions) can take actions on your behalf. You remain the final decision-maker; Apogee functions more like a chief-of-staff or strategic advisor than an assistant that just files things away.

Practically, that means Apogee is:
- **Voice and text enabled** — you can talk to it or type to it.
- **Context-aware** — it pulls from your projects, market research, validation work (like the Perfume Vending project), legal/governance notes, and daily rhythms rather than treating every question in isolation.
- **Action-capable** — under safety rules, it can perform tasks, not just describe them.
- **Built to avoid becoming "just a filing cabinet"** — the explicit goal is synthesis and judgment support, not passive storage.

Right now it's an active build — you're the one developing it (per the Builder Agent master prompt), working from your existing vault architecture rather than rebuilding from scratch.

If you meant something narrower — like what Apogee is *currently capable of* today, or what it means in the context of a specific project — let me know and I'll give you that instead."

### Apogee Interaction
* **Command:** "I have a case for a company that is employing AI and I need help solving it."
* **Action:** "I can help with that — but I want to make sure I actually understand the case before diving in, since "AI adoption case" could mean a lot of different things (business strategy, ethics/governance, technical implementation, hiring/organizational design, competitive analysis, etc.).

Can you give me the details? Specifically it'd help to know:

1. **What's the actual question or problem** the case is asking you to solve (e.g., should the company adopt AI, how should they implement it, what went wrong, what's the recommendation being requested)?
2. **What information you already have** — background on the company, the AI system in question, constraints, stakeholders, data provided.
3. **What format the answer needs to take** — a written analysis, a recommendation memo, a decision framework, talking points, etc.

Once I have the actual case content, I can help you work through it properly rather than guessing at a generic AI-strategy answer. If it's a written case document, feel free to paste it in or summarize the key facts."

### Apogee Interaction
* **Command:** "Read the AI_Governance_Investigation_One_Pager.md"
* **Action:** "# AI Governance Investigation — Working One-Pager

## Purpose
A practical investigation method for understanding a real-world AI governance problem before reaching conclusions or selecting controls.

**Learn → Question → Test → Decide → Build**

## 1. Understand the Situation
Establish:
- What is happening?
- What AI system, process, decision, or capability is involved?
- Where does AI enter the process?
- Who is involved and who is affected?
- What outcome or concern has actually been observed?
- What does the organisation believe the problem is?

Do not assume the initial description is the complete problem.

## 2. Separate the Starting Information
Distinguish:
- **Facts** — what is established
- **Claims** — what someone says is true
- **Assumptions** — what is being taken for granted
- **Evidence** — what supports a claim
- **Unknowns** — what is not established
- **Questions** — what needs investigation

## 3. Investigate
Turn the initial situation into specific questions.

### AI system
- What does the system actually do?
- What data does it use?
- What decisions or outputs does it influence?
- What happens to its output afterwards?

### People and impact
- Who benefits?
- Who may be harmed?
- Who decides?
- Who can challenge the outcome?
- What happens if the system is wrong?
- At what scale could the effects occur?

### Evidence
- What evidence exists?
- Where did it come from?
- How reliable is it?
- What does it establish?
- What does it not establish?
- What information is missing?

## 4. Apply Relevant Governance Knowledge
Use the sources appropriate to the situation:
- laws and regulations
- official guidance
- standards
- organisational policies
- academic research
- technical documentation
- documented incidents
- ethical frameworks
- sector-specific guidance

A framework or principle is not automatically applicable. Ask what it actually tells us about this particular situation.

## 5. Define the Specific Problem
Move from a broad concern to a precise question.

For example:

**“The AI may be unfair.”**

becomes:

- What does “fair” mean here?
- Which groups are being compared?
- What outcome is being measured?
- How is it being measured?
- Could other factors explain the difference?
- What evidence supports the claim?
- What remains unknown?

**A principle is not yet a finding.**

## 6. Evaluate Evidence
For important conclusions, distinguish:

**What we know**  
**What we believe**  
**What the evidence supports**  
**What remains uncertain**

Research informs the conclusion; it does not determine the conclusion automatically.

Questions should become more specific as understanding increases.

## 7. Identify Possible Controls
Only after the problem is sufficiently understood consider responses.

**Principle → specific problem → definition → measurement → evidence → possible control → evaluation**

Controls may be:
- technical
- organisational
- procedural
- contractual
- governance-based
- human oversight
- monitoring
- validation
- documentation
- correction/redress mechanisms

The appropriate response depends on the actual risk and context.

## 8. Test the Proposed Response
Ask:
- Does the control address the identified problem?
- What evidence would show that it works?
- What new risks could it introduce?
- What trade-offs exist?
- What assumptions does it depend on?
- What happens when it fails?
- How will it be monitored after deployment?

## 9. State What Is Established

### Established
What the evidence supports.

### Supported interpretation
What the available evidence reasonably suggests.

### Unresolved
What cannot yet be established.

### Further investigation
What evidence or analysis is still required.

### Possible actions
Options that could be considered, with their rationale and trade-offs.

## Investigation Loop

**Understand → Identify facts/claims/assumptions/evidence/unknowns → Question → Investigate → Gather evidence → Refine the problem → Apply relevant governance knowledge → Test possible controls → Evaluate → Establish findings → Identify unknowns → Further investigation/action → Learn and revisit**

## Five Questions to Keep Returning To

**Who benefits?**  
**Who is harmed?**  
**Who decides?**  
**Who can challenge?**  
**At what scale?**

Across the lifecycle:

**Where is responsibility being exercised — design, deployment, use, monitoring, correction, or redesign?**

## Working Principle

**Understand first. Investigate second. Conclude last.**

The purpose is to establish:

**What happened → what we know → what the evidence supports → what we do not know → what needs investigating → what options exist → what should be decided by the responsible human or organisation.**

The framework supports professional judgment; it does not replace it."
